I was managing a very heavy caseload at my clinic while also trying to care for my aging aunt who has a lot of medical problems. The stress of balancing both just seemed to trigger this shut-down feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life? If yes, have you made any plans?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any thoughts of hurting myself or ending my life, and I certainly haven't made any plans. Even though I feel incredibly empty and shut down inside, those kinds of thoughts haven't crossed my mind.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you also had classic depression symptoms like feeling sad most of the day, losing interest or pleasure in things, changes in sleep or appetite, or feeling worthless or guilty? Or does it feel more like emotional exhaustion and detachment mainly tied to the ongoing stress?\n\nPatient: It definitely feels more like a profound emotional blankness and detachment rather than active sadness or guilt. It’s as if the volume has been turned down on all my emotions, leaving me on autopilot under the heavy weight of my work caseload and caring for my aunt.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings lift when you’re away from work or taking time off, or do they stay about the same even outside of work and caregiving? Also, do you mainly feel cynical and emotionally drained by your work, or distressed across many parts of life?\n\nPatient: No, these feelings don't lift when I'm away; they stay exactly the same. It's not just work-related burnout, but a heavy, shut-down feeling that has taken over my entire life. I've completely stopped doing yoga, I'm ignoring my friends, and I can't even keep up with basic things like cleaning or buying groceries.\n\nDoctor: Because this started after major stress, I want to understand how severe it is: are you still able to go to work and manage basic daily tasks like bathing, eating, and paying bills, or are these becoming very hard or impossible? Have you had any psychotic symptoms, like hearing voices or having beliefs others say aren’t true?\n\nPatient: Managing daily life has become incredibly difficult; I've been written up at work, had clients reassigned, and I'm completely neglecting basic chores like cleaning and groceries while isolating at home. On top of that, lately I can't shake the feeling that people at work and at my aunt's hospital are secretly evaluating or investigating me through hidden hints in meetings and emails, even though my supervisor insists it's just routine feedback.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that people are secretly evaluating or investigating you, are you able to recognize that this might just be stress and not actually true, or does it feel completely certain to you? Have you also heard or seen things that other people do not?\n\nPatient: While a small part of me logically knows it might just be the stress, the feeling that I'm being secretly investigated feels incredibly real and convincing, making it very hard to shake off. I haven't heard or seen anything that others don't, though; that hasn't happened to me.\n\nDoctor: When you think people are secretly investigating you, is it mainly because you feel you’ve failed, done something wrong, or deserve blame? Or does it feel unrelated to depression and more like others are targeting you for some other reason?\n\nPatient: It doesn't feel like it's about guilt or that I've done something wrong; it feels much more like a coordinated effort where I'm being targeted. I believe they are evaluating me for some sort of secret program or investigation. I keep picking up on hints they are dropping in staff meetings, emails, and even when I visit my aunt at the hospital.\n\nDoctor: Even if the work and caregiving stress were removed, do you think the belief that you’re being secretly investigated would still feel true? Or does it seem mainly tied to feeling overwhelmed and shut down by these stressors?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest; I haven't thought about it that way. Right now, the stress of work, caring for my aunt, and the feeling that I'm being investigated are all so completely tangled together that I can't separate them.\n\nDoctor: Since the feeling that you’re being secretly investigated first began, has it been present for less than 1 month, or has it continued for more than 1 month? Around when did it start?"

On top of that, lately I can't shake the feeling that people at work and at my aunt's hospital are secretly evaluating or investigating me through hidden hints in meetings and emails, even though my supervisor insists it's just routine feedback.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that people are secretly evaluating or investigating you, are you able to recognize that this might just be stress and not actually true, or does it feel completely certain to you? Have you also heard or seen things that other people do not?\n\nPatient: While a small part of me logically knows it might just be the stress, the feeling that I'm being secretly investigated feels incredibly real and convincing, making it very hard to shake off. I haven't heard or seen anything that others don't, though; that hasn't happened to me.\n\nDoctor: When you think people are secretly investigating you, is it mainly because you feel you’ve failed, done something wrong, or deserve blame? Or does it feel unrelated to depression and more like others are targeting you for some other reason?\n\nPatient: It doesn't feel like it's about guilt or that I've done something wrong; it feels much more like a coordinated effort where I'm being targeted. I believe they are evaluating me for some sort of secret program or investigation. I keep picking up on hints they are dropping in staff meetings, emails, and even when I visit my aunt at the hospital.\n\nDoctor: Even if the work and caregiving stress were removed, do you think the belief that you’re being secretly investigated would still feel true? Or does it seem mainly tied to feeling overwhelmed and shut down by these stressors?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest; I haven't thought about it that way. Right now, the stress of work, caring for my aunt, and the feeling that I'm being investigated are all so completely tangled together that I can't separate them.\n\nDoctor: Since the feeling that you’re being secretly investigated first began, has it been present for less than 1 month, or has it continued for more than 1 month? Around when did it start?\n\nPatient: It has been going on for more than a month, probably for about the last two months now. It started around the time my aunt's hospital visits got more frequent, and it's just been this constant, exhausting feeling that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: During these last two months, have you also had a clear depressed mood most of the day or strong feelings of worthlessness, hopelessness, or guilt nearly every day? Or has the main problem been the belief that you’re being investigated without those classic depression feelings?"

I haven't heard or seen anything that others don't, though; that hasn't happened to me.\n\nDoctor: When you think people are secretly investigating you, is it mainly because you feel you’ve failed, done something wrong, or deserve blame? Or does it feel unrelated to depression and more like others are targeting you for some other reason?\n\nPatient: It doesn't feel like it's about guilt or that I've done something wrong; it feels much more like a coordinated effort where I'm being targeted. I believe they are evaluating me for some sort of secret program or investigation. I keep picking up on hints they are dropping in staff meetings, emails, and even when I visit my aunt at the hospital.\n\nDoctor: Even if the work and caregiving stress were removed, do you think the belief that you’re being secretly investigated would still feel true? Or does it seem mainly tied to feeling overwhelmed and shut down by these stressors?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest; I haven't thought about it that way. Right now, the stress of work, caring for my aunt, and the feeling that I'm being investigated are all so completely tangled together that I can't separate them.\n\nDoctor: Since the feeling that you’re being secretly investigated first began, has it been present for less than 1 month, or has it continued for more than 1 month? Around when did it start?\n\nPatient: It has been going on for more than a month, probably for about the last two months now. It started around the time my aunt's hospital visits got more frequent, and it's just been this constant, exhausting feeling that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: During these last two months, have you also had a clear depressed mood most of the day or strong feelings of worthlessness, hopelessness, or guilt nearly every day? Or has the main problem been the belief that you’re being investigated without those classic depression feelings?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt those classic feelings of guilt or worthlessness; it's more like a profound emptiness where my emotions and drive are completely blank and shut down. The main, most distressing thing these past two months has definitely been this unsettling belief that I'm being secretly investigated, on top of having no motivation at all.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, had you ever had similar beliefs that people were targeting, monitoring, or investigating you at any other time in your life? And if your work and caregiving stress were to improve, do you think this belief would fade, or would it still feel true?"
  }

No, I have never had any thoughts or beliefs like this at any other point in my life before these past two months. And because it feels so incredibly real to me right now, it's hard to imagine it would just fade away even if my stress improved, though I'm not entirely sure.

I've completely stopped doing yoga, I'm ignoring my friends, and I can't even keep up with basic things like cleaning or buying groceries.\n\nDoctor: Because this started after major stress, I want to understand how severe it is: are you still able to go to work and manage basic daily tasks like bathing, eating, and paying bills, or are these becoming very hard or impossible? Have you had any psychotic symptoms, like hearing voices or having beliefs others say aren’t true?\n\nPatient: Managing daily life has become incredibly difficult; I've been written up at work, had clients reassigned, and I'm completely neglecting basic chores like cleaning and groceries while isolating at home. On top of that, lately I can't shake the feeling that people at work and at my aunt's hospital are secretly evaluating or investigating me through hidden hints in meetings and emails, even though my supervisor insists it's just routine feedback.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that people are secretly evaluating or investigating you, are you able to recognize that this might just be stress and not actually true, or does it feel completely certain to you? Have you also heard or seen things that other people do not?\n\nPatient: While a small part of me logically knows it might just be the stress, the feeling that I'm being secretly investigated feels incredibly real and convincing, making it very hard to shake off. I haven't heard or seen anything that others don't, though; that hasn't happened to me.\n\nDoctor: When you think people are secretly investigating you, is it mainly because you feel you’ve failed, done something wrong, or deserve blame? Or does it feel unrelated to depression and more like others are targeting you for some other reason?\n\nPatient: It doesn't feel like it's about guilt or that I've done something wrong; it feels much more like a coordinated effort where I'm being targeted. I believe they are evaluating me for some sort of secret program or investigation. I keep picking up on hints they are dropping in staff meetings, emails, and even when I visit my aunt at the hospital.\n\nDoctor: Even if the work and caregiving stress were removed, do you think the belief that you’re being secretly investigated would still feel true? Or does it seem mainly tied to feeling overwhelmed and shut down by these stressors?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest; I haven't thought about it that way. Right now, the stress of work, caring for my aunt, and the feeling that I'm being investigated are all so completely tangled together that I can't separate them.\n\nDoctor: Since the feeling that you’re being secretly investigated first began, has it been present for less than 1 month, or has it continued for more than 1 month? Around when did it start?\n\nPatient: It has been going on for more than a month, probably for about the last two months now. It started around the time my aunt's hospital visits got more frequent, and it's just been this constant, exhausting feeling that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: During these last two months, have you also had a clear depressed mood most of the day or strong feelings of worthlessness, hopelessness, or guilt nearly every day? Or has the main problem been the belief that you’re being investigated without those classic depression feelings?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt those classic feelings of guilt or worthlessness; it's more like a profound emptiness where my emotions and drive are completely blank and shut down. The main, most distressing thing these past two months has definitely been this unsettling belief that I'm being secretly investigated, on top of having no motivation at all.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, had you ever had similar beliefs that people were targeting, monitoring, or investigating you at any other time in your life? And if your work and caregiving stress were to improve, do you think this belief would fade, or would it still feel true?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts or beliefs like this at any other point in my life before these past two months. And because it feels so incredibly real to me right now, it's hard to imagine it would just fade away even if my stress improved, though I'm not entirely sure.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this belief that you’re being investigated, are your thinking and daily functioning mostly normal, or have you also had major problems like disorganized speech, confusion, or very odd behavior? Has anyone close to you noticed those kinds of changes?"

This is definitely the first time I've experienced these symptoms and felt so completely shut down. Or did they come on without any clear trigger?\n\nPatient: It really started building about eight months ago when things got incredibly overwhelming. I was managing a very heavy caseload at my clinic while also trying to care for my aging aunt who has a lot of medical problems. If yes, have you made any plans?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any thoughts of hurting myself or ending my life, and I certainly haven't made any plans. Even though I feel incredibly empty and shut down inside, those kinds of thoughts haven't crossed my mind.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you also had classic depression symptoms like feeling sad most of the day, losing interest or pleasure in things, changes in sleep or appetite, or feeling worthless or guilty? Or does it feel more like emotional exhaustion and detachment mainly tied to the ongoing stress?\n\nPatient: It definitely feels more like a profound emotional blankness and detachment rather than active sadness or guilt. It’s as if the volume has been turned down on all my emotions, leaving me on autopilot under the heavy weight of my work caseload and caring for my aunt.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings lift when you’re away from work or taking time off, or do they stay about the same even outside of work and caregiving? Also, do you mainly feel cynical and emotionally drained by your work, or distressed across many parts of life?\n\nPatient: No, these feelings don't lift when I'm away; they stay exactly the same. I've completely stopped doing yoga, I'm ignoring my friends, and I can't even keep up with basic things like cleaning or buying groceries.\n\nDoctor: Because this started after major stress, I want to understand how severe it is: are you still able to go to work and manage basic daily tasks like bathing, eating, and paying bills, or are these becoming very hard or impossible? Have you had any psychotic symptoms, like hearing voices or having beliefs others say aren’t true?\n\nPatient: Managing daily life has become incredibly difficult; I've been written up at work, had clients reassigned, and I'm completely neglecting basic chores like cleaning and groceries while isolating at home. On top of that, lately I can't shake the feeling that people at work and at my aunt's hospital are secretly evaluating or investigating me through hidden hints in meetings and emails, even though my supervisor insists it's just routine feedback.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that people are secretly evaluating or investigating you, are you able to recognize that this might just be stress and not actually true, or does it feel completely certain to you? Have you also heard or seen things that other people do not?\n\nPatient: While a small part of me logically knows it might just be the stress, the feeling that I'm being secretly investigated feels incredibly real and convincing, making it very hard to shake off. I haven't heard or seen anything that others don't, though; that hasn't happened to me.\n\nDoctor: When you think people are secretly investigating you, is it mainly because you feel you’ve failed, done something wrong, or deserve blame? Or does it feel unrelated to depression and more like others are targeting you for some other reason?\n\nPatient: It doesn't feel like it's about guilt or that I've done something wrong; it feels much more like a coordinated effort where I'm being targeted. I believe they are evaluating me for some sort of secret program or investigation. I keep picking up on hints they are dropping in staff meetings, emails, and even when I visit my aunt at the hospital.\n\nDoctor: Even if the work and caregiving stress were removed, do you think the belief that you’re being secretly investigated would still feel true? Or does it seem mainly tied to feeling overwhelmed and shut down by these stressors?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest; I haven't thought about it that way. Right now, the stress of work, caring for my aunt, and the feeling that I'm being investigated are all so completely tangled together that I can't separate them.\n\nDoctor: Since the feeling that you’re being secretly investigated first began, has it been present for less than 1 month, or has it continued for more than 1 month? Around when did it start?\n\nPatient: It has been going on for more than a month, probably for about the last two months now. It started around the time my aunt's hospital visits got more frequent, and it's just been this constant, exhausting feeling that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: During these last two months, have you also had a clear depressed mood most of the day or strong feelings of worthlessness, hopelessness, or guilt nearly every day? The main, most distressing thing these past two months has definitely been this unsettling belief that I'm being secretly investigated, on top of having no motivation at all.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, had you ever had similar beliefs that people were targeting, monitoring, or investigating you at any other time in your life? And if your work and caregiving stress were to improve, do you think this belief would fade, or would it still feel true?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts or beliefs like this at any other point in my life before these past two months. And because it feels so incredibly real to me right now, it's hard to imagine it would just fade away even if my stress improved, though I'm not entirely sure.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this belief that you’re being investigated, are your thinking and daily functioning mostly normal, or have you also had major problems like disorganized speech, confusion, or very odd behavior? Has anyone close to you noticed those kinds of changes?\n\nPatient: My daily functioning is severely impacted—I’m slipping at work with missed deadlines and reassigned clients, and completely neglecting my home life and friends. I don't even realize I'm doing it until someone physically taps my arm or calls my name to get me moving again.\n\nDoctor: From the very first time you noticed people were secretly investigating you until now, has there ever been a full month when that belief or any other psychotic symptoms were present most days even without feeling emotionally shut down? Also, in total, have these psychotic symptoms been going on for less than 6 months or 6 months or more?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had those thoughts without the emotional shutdown, because that blank, shut-down feeling started about eight months ago and has been constant ever since. The belief that I'm being investigated only began about two months ago, so those symptoms have been going on for less than six months in total. Since the shutdown has been continuous, I've only ever experienced the investigations while also feeling completely shut down.\n\nDoctor: Since the emotional shutdown began eight months ago, have there been continuous signs of illness the whole time, with the last two months including the suspicious beliefs and freezing episodes? Or was there a period of full recovery in between?"

And because it feels so incredibly real to me right now, it's hard to imagine it would just fade away even if my stress improved, though I'm not entirely sure.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this belief that you’re being investigated, are your thinking and daily functioning mostly normal, or have you also had major problems like disorganized speech, confusion, or very odd behavior? Has anyone close to you noticed those kinds of changes?\n\nPatient: My daily functioning is severely impacted—I’m slipping at work with missed deadlines and reassigned clients, and completely neglecting my home life and friends. My coworkers have also noticed some odd behavior, telling me that I will suddenly freeze and stand completely still in the hallways or copy room, staring into space for minutes. I don't even realize I'm doing it until someone physically taps my arm or calls my name to get me moving again.\n\nDoctor: From the very first time you noticed people were secretly investigating you until now, has there ever been a full month when that belief or any other psychotic symptoms were present most days even without feeling emotionally shut down? Also, in total, have these psychotic symptoms been going on for less than 6 months or 6 months or more?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had those thoughts without the emotional shutdown, because that blank, shut-down feeling started about eight months ago and has been constant ever since. The belief that I'm being investigated only began about two months ago, so those symptoms have been going on for less than six months in total. Since the shutdown has been continuous, I've only ever experienced the investigations while also feeling completely shut down.\n\nDoctor: Since the emotional shutdown began eight months ago, have there been continuous signs of illness the whole time, with the last two months including the suspicious beliefs and freezing episodes? Or was there a period of full recovery in between?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has been completely continuous over the past eight months without any period of recovery at all. The heavy, shut-down feeling and the decline in my functioning have just steadily built up the entire time. The suspicious thoughts about being evaluated and the freezing episodes are indeed more recent additions, only starting within the last two months."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
